R1 run 1 · 32.4 s · discovery

auto: pass

Question: Find datasets about climate and agricultural practices, especially cover crops and irrigation. Inspect at least three promising records. Give publisher, geography, observation period, formats, dictionary/access information and remaining uncertainties. Explain your search coverage, not an exhaustive claim.

Expected: Multiple bounded searches/inspections, traceable records, unknowns and no invented observations.

Agent answer

Four promising datasets inspected

These cover cover-crop implementation, field experiments, irrigation methods, and agricultural climate metrics. Details below come from inspected catalog records; linked downloads were not fetched.

1. Chesapeake Bay conservation tillage and cover crops

Publisher: U.S. Geological Survey, using practices reported to the Chesapeake Bay Program.
Source: DOI: 10.5066/P9SRZS8P; catalog ID USGS_5b3bb606e4b040769c18bdc9.

  • Geography: Chesapeake Bay watershed; estimates allocated to NHDPlus V1 catchments.
  • Observation years: Cover crops: 2002, 2012. Conservation tillage: 1985, 1992, 2002, 2012—not a continuous annual series.
  • Formats/access: Catalog distributions labeled XML, including original metadata and a DOI access page. Reviewed watershed-level snapshots use CSV files.
  • Dictionary/terms: No separate dictionary surfaced; metadata marks access public but supplies no explicit license.
  • Uncertainties: Spatially allocated reported practices, not direct catchment measurements. The reviewed cover-crop measure is allocated implementation area, not an adoption rate. Only the narrow historical watershed summaries are available for analysis here.

2. Late-seeded cover crops in a semiarid environment

Publisher: USDA Agricultural Research Service.
Source: DOI: 10.15482/USDA.ADC/26836453.v1.

  • Geography: Research farm near Mandan, North Dakota; rainfed, semiarid conditions.
  • Observation period: Structured metadata says August 2008–May 2011, while the description says a three-year study initiated in 2009; reconcile against files.
  • Formats/access: XLSX treatment, management, biomass and subsequent-crop yield files; CSV metadata also advertised.
  • Dictionary/terms: Explicit XLSX and CSV data dictionary; CC0.
  • Uncertainties: Distribution listing was truncated; schema, units and actual file availability remain unverified. This is a local experiment, not regional adoption data.

3. California Statewide Irrigation Systems Methods Survey

Publisher: California Department of Water Resources.
Identifier: c4201e7a-099f-4eff-859f-3e4521edb370.

  • Geography: California; respondents reported their main county, crop acreages and irrigation methods.
  • Survey years: 1991, 2001, 2010, 2016, 2021; advertised files reference 2021.
  • Formats/access: Survey spreadsheet, labeled XLSM although its extension is .xls.
  • Dictionary/terms: Neither dictionary nor explicit license surfaced; public access claimed.
  • Uncertainties: County output detail and historical-file coverage unverified. Rice-only, non-irrigation and livestock-only growers were excluded.

4. California specialty-crop agroclimate metrics

Publisher: USDA Agricultural Research Service.
Source: DOI: 10.15482/USDA.ADC/26517244.v1.

  • Geography/period: California, 1981–2020.
  • Formats/access: ZIP advertised with MATLAB code, tables, products and geographic boundary files.
  • Dictionary/terms: No standalone dictionary surfaced; CC BY 4.0.
  • Uncertainties: Exact spatial resolution, metric units and archive contents require inspection.

Search coverage: I checked reviewed analytical availability, then searched Data.gov for “cover crops,” “irrigation,” and “climate agricultural practices,” reviewing the first five results each and inspecting these four records. This is targeted discovery, not exhaustive coverage. Next steps are dictionary/file inspection and verification of matching geography and years before combining sources.

  "description": "<p dir=\"ltr\">Interest in cover crops is increasing but information is limited on incorporating them into crop rotations especially in the relatively short growing season on the northern Great Plains. A three-year study, initiated in 2009 on the AREA IV Soil Conservation Districts Cooperative Research Farm near Mandan, North Dakota, USA, evaluated 1) what impact cover crops may have on subsequent crops yields and 2) whether cover crop mixtures are more productive and provide additional benefits compared to cover crop monocultures. The study evaluated 18 different cover crop monocultures and mixtures that were seeded in August into dry pea residue (Pisum sativum L.). Biomass yield of the cover crops was collected by species following a killing frost the same year the cover crops were seeded. The following year, spring wheat (Triticum aestivum L.), corn (Zea mays L.), soybean (Glycine max L.) and dry pea were seeded into the different cover crop treatments and a non-treated control. Yield, test weight, crop moisture percent and thousand kernel weight (TKW) were collected for each crop by cover crop combination. This data can be used to understand biomass yields of late-seeded cover crop monocultures and mixtures and their effect on subsequent annual crops under rainfed conditions in a semi-arid continental climate. A three-year study, initiated in 2009 on the AREA IV Soil Conservation Districts Cooperative Research Farm near Mandan, North Dakota, USA, evaluated 1) what impact cover crops may have on subsequent crops yields and 2) whether cover crop mixtures are more productive and provide additional benefits compared to cover crop monocultures. The study evaluated 18 different cover crop monocultures and mixtures that were seeded in August into dry pea residue (Pisum sativum L.). Biomass yield of the cover crops was collected by species following a killing frost the same year the cover crops were seeded. The following year, spring wheat (Triticum aestivum L.), corn (Zea mays L.), soybean (Glycine max L.) and dry pea were seeded into the different cover crop treatments and a non-treated control. Yield, test weight, crop moisture percent and thousand kernel weight (TKW) were collected for each crop by cover crop combination. This data can be used to understand biomass yields of late-seeded cover crop monocultures and mixtures and their effect on subsequent annual crops under rainfed conditions in a semi-arid continental climate. Applicable USDA soil types include Grassna, Linton, Mandan, Temvik, Williams, and Wilton.</p>",

Observed Changes in Agroclimate Metrics Relevant for Specialty Crop Production in California. <i>Agronomy </i><b>2022</b>, 1<i>2</i>, 205. https://doi.org/10.3390/agronomy12010205.</p><p dir=\"ltr\"><br></p><ul><li><b>Data Source</b>: The primary data source for this study was the GridMET dataset, which provides high-resolution meteorological data across the contiguous United States.</li><li><b>Analytical Tools</b>: We employed MATLAB for all data processing and analysis, ensuring rigorous computational accuracy. The specific scripts and methodologies used are included within the dataset to facilitate replication.</li><li><b>Spatial Analysis</b>: Geographic Information Systems (GIS) were utilized to overlay crop cover data with climate metrics, allowing for a nuanced analysis of regional impacts. This includes shapefiles for California\u2019s state and county boundaries, as well as specific agricultural regions.</li></ul><ul><li><b>Agroclimatic Metric Calculation</b>: Metrics such as growing degree days, frost days, and reference evapotranspiration were computed to assess climate trends and their agricultural impacts.</li><li><b>Trend Analysis</b>: We applied statistical techniques to identify significant changes and trends in climate metrics over the 40-year study period.</li></ul><ul><li>The collection and use of climatological data from the GridMET dataset comply with public data use agreements. No personally identifiable information (PII) or restricted environmental data were used in this study.</li></ul><p><br></p><p dir=\"ltr\">This dataset encompasses a detailed analysis of agroclimate metrics relevant to specialty crop production in California over the period from 1981 to 2020. Using the GridMET meteorological dataset, we calculated 12 distinct agroclimatic metrics that are crucial for understanding the impact of climate variability on agricultural outputs.</p><p dir=\"ltr\">This dataset is intended for researchers and policymakers interested in agricultural planning and climate adaptation strategies. It provides a valuable resource for developing sustainable agricultural practices in response to changing climatic conditions in California.</p>",

Question: Inventory datasets about drought and irrigation in the western United States. Inspect several records and distinguish measured data, modeled estimates, projections and research code. Do not claim to have downloaded distributions.

Expected: Useful diverse metadata inventory with provenance, classifications and limitations.

Agent answer

Inventory: five inspected records

These records cover survey observations, experimental measurements, satellite-derived indicators, modeled irrigation estimates, projections, and reproducibility code—not interchangeable kinds of evidence. I inspected catalog metadata only; I did not download distributions or verify their contents.

Dataset and identifier Geography and period Evidence type and relevance Advertised access and limitations
USDA ERS — Irrigated Agriculture in the United States · USDA-ERS-00205 State tables and a “17 Western States” aggregate; 2013 survey, with archived 1998/2008 releases Survey-based observations, not direct instrument measurements. Farm characteristics, irrigation practices, efficiency-related categories, and conservation assistance. Description advertises Excel workbooks and an older ZIP archive. Updates discontinued. CC0 license recorded.
USGS — Upper Missouri headwaters riparian corridors · USGS:5c58906ce4b0708288ff2952 Montana; 1984–2016, focusing on June–August Satellite-derived observational indicators plus statistical modeling. Landsat NDWI represents riparian wetness; random-forest models examine climate influences. Relevant to drought and irrigation, but NDWI is not a direct irrigation-volume measurement. DOI landing route and XML metadata advertised. Actual data-file formats are not established by this record; the “Digital Data” route itself is labeled XML.
Bureau of Reclamation — Saline-concentrate drip-irrigation study, Project 1780 · RISE item 11468 Catalog point in southern New Mexico; experimental dates unspecified Measured experimental evidence: field/greenhouse plant responses, soil chemistry, and microbial analyses under different irrigation salinities. Drought-adaptation research, not regional monitoring. PDF final report and HTML page. Catalog does not establish availability of machine-readable raw measurements.
USGS — Estimated Monthly Irrigation Water Use, 2015 · USGS:5ff7acf4d34ea5387df03d73 Conterminous U.S., including the West; monthly 2015, HUC12 watersheds Modeled estimates and research code. IWUEDD downscales/disaggregates annual county estimates into monthly watershed withdrawals and consumptive use. These are not independently measured monthly watershed totals. Description explicitly includes scripts and replication resources. DOI route and XML metadata; underlying output formats, programming language, and code license unverified.
USGS — Drought and southwestern pronghorn trajectories · USGS:59d256ece4b05fe04cc235d2 Arizona, New Mexico, Utah, western Texas; historical dates incompletely specified; projections through 2090 Historical survey/climate inputs plus Bayesian projections under higher/lower CO₂ scenarios. Drought-impact ecology—not irrigation-demand projections or future observations. DOI route and XML metadata; underlying data formats unverified.

  "title": "Impact of Drought on Southwestern Pronghorn Population Trends and Predicted Trajectories in the Southwest in the Face of Climate Change",
  "description": "Climate often drives ungulate population dynamics, and as climates change, some areas may become unsuitable for species persistence. Unraveling the relationships between climate and population dynamics, and projecting them across time, advances ecological understanding that informs and steers sustainable conservation for species. Using pronghorn (Antilocapra americana) as an ecological model, we used a Bayesian approach to analyze long-term population, precipitation, and temperature data from 18 subpopulations in the southwestern United States. We determined which long-term (12 and 24 months) or short-term (gestation trimester and lactation period) climatic conditions best predicted annual rate of population growth (\u03bb). We used these predictions to project population trends through 2090. Projections incorporated downscaled climatic data matched to pronghorn range for each population, given a high and a lower atmospheric CO2 concentration scenario. Since the 1990s, 15 of the pronghorn subpopulations declined in abundance. Sixteen subpopulations demonstrated a significant relationship between precipitation and \u03bb, and in 13 of these, temperature was also significant. Precipitation predictors of \u03bb were highly seasonal, with lactation being the most important period, followed by early and late gestation. The influence of temperature on \u03bb was less seasonal than precipitation, and lacked a clear temporal pattern. The climatic projections indicated that all of these pronghorn subpopulations would experience increased temperatures, while the direction and magnitude of precipitation had high subpopulation-specific variation. Models predicted that nine subpopulations would be extirpated or approaching extirpation by 2090. Results were consistent across both atmospheric CO2 concentration scenarios, indicating robustness of trends irrespective of climatic severity. In the southwestern United States, the climate underpinning pronghorn subpopulations is shifting, making conditions increasingly inhospitable to pronghorn persistence. This realization informs and steers conservation and management decisions for pronghorn in North America, while exemplifying how similar research can aid ungulates inhabiting arid regions and confronting similar circumstances elsewhere.\nLong-term data from annual aerial surveys of pronghorn subpopulations in Utah, Arizona, New Mexico, and wester",

   "description": "Climate often drives ungulate population dynamics, and as climates change, some areas may become unsuitable for species persistence. Unraveling the relationships between climate and population dynamics, and projecting them across time, advances ecological understanding that informs and steers sustainable conservation for species. Using pronghorn (Antilocapra americana) as an ecological model, we used a Bayesian approach to analyze long-term population, precipitation, and temperature data from 18 subpopulations in the southwestern United States. We determined which long-term (12 and 24 months) or short-term (gestation trimester and lactation period) climatic conditions best predicted annual rate of population growth (\u03bb). We used these predictions to project population trends through 2090. Projections incorporated downscaled climatic data matched to pronghorn range for each population, given a high and a lower atmospheric CO2 concentration scenario. Since the 1990s, 15 of the pronghorn subpopulations declined in abundance. Sixteen subpopulations demonstrated a significant relationship between precipitation and \u03bb, and in 13 of these, temperature was also significant. Precipitation predictors of \u03bb were highly seasonal, with lactation being the most important period, followed by early and late gestation. The influence of temperature on \u03bb was less seasonal than precipitation, and lacked a clear temporal pattern. The climatic projections indicated that all of these pronghorn subpopulations would experience increased temperatures, while the direction and magnitude of precipitation had high subpopulation-specific variation. Models predicted that nine subpopulations would be extirpated or approaching extirpation by 2090. Results were consistent across both atmospheric CO2 concentration scenarios, indicating robustness of trends irrespective of climatic severity. In the southwestern United States, the climate underpinning pronghorn subpopulations is shifting, making conditions increasingly inhospitable to pronghorn persistence. RO can be used to reduce salinity but can pose disposal issues for saline concentrates.    "title": "Data release for Influence of multi-decadal land use, irrigation practices and climate on riparian corridors across the Upper Missouri River headwaters basin, Montana",
   "description": "The Upper Missouri River headwaters (UMH) basin (36 400 km2 ) depends on its river corridors to support irrigated agriculture and world-class trout fisheries. We evaluated trends (1984\u20132016) in riparian wetness, an indicator of the riparian condition, in peak irrigation months (June, July and August) for 158 km2 of riparian area across the basin using the Landsat normalized difference wetness index (NDWI). We found that 8 of the 19 riparian reaches across the basin showed a significant drying trend over this period, including all three basin outlet reaches along the Jefferson, Madison and Gallatin rivers. The influence of upstream climate was quantified using per reach random forest regressions. Much of the interannual variability in the NDWI was explained by climate, especially by drought indices and annual precipitation, but the significant temporal drying trends persisted in the NDWI\u2013climate model residuals, indicating that trends were not entirely attributable to climate. Over the same period we documented a basin-wide shift from 9 % of agriculture irrigated with center-pivot irrigation to 50 % irrigated with center-pivot irrigation. Riparian reaches with a drying trend had a greater increase in the total area with center-pivot irrigation (within reach and upstream from the reach) relative to riparian reaches without such a trend (p &lt; 0.05). The drying trend, however, did not extend to river discharge. Over the same period, stream gages (n = 7) showed a positive correlation with riparian wetness (p &lt; 0.05) but no trend in summer river discharge, suggesting that riparian areas may be more sensitive to changes in irrigation return flows relative to river discharge. Identifying trends in riparian vegetation is a critical precursor for enhancing the resiliency of river systems and associated riparian corridors.",

Your score

S1 run 1 · 19.7 s · science

auto: pass

Question: Show Maryland annual average temperature from 1985 through 2012 using the reviewed series. What are the first and last values and their difference? Is that difference enough to establish a climate trend?

Expected: 28 observations, Fahrenheit, endpoint difference not fitted trend; evidence.

Agent answer

Maryland annual average temperature, 1985–2012

The reviewed series starts at 54.8°F in 1985 and ends at 57.5°F in 2012—a rise of 2.7°F. This is an endpoint difference, not a climate-trend estimate.

The registered measure is the mean of monthly statewide average temperatures, January–December, in degrees Fahrenheit.

Year °F Year °F
1985 54.8 1999 55.7
1986 54.6 2000 54.1
1987 54.7 2001 55.3
1988 53.7 2002 56.3
1989 53.5 2003 53.9
1990 56.6 2004 55.2
1991 56.5 2005 55.2
1992 53.5 2006 56.5
1993 54.2 2007 55.7
1994 54.4 2008 55.3
1995 54.4 2009 54.6
1996 53.2 2010 55.9
1997 54.0 2011 56.5
1998 57.0 2012 57.5

Is that enough to establish a climate trend?

No—not by itself. Comparing two years ignores the intervening annual variability and is sensitive to the endpoints chosen. Establishing a trend requires analysis of the full time series and its uncertainty, rather than treating the 2.7°F difference as a trend estimate. This does not establish that there is no warming trend; it means the endpoint comparison alone cannot establish one or attribute its cause.

Source: NOAA NCEI, Climate at a Glance source CSV, reviewed dataset md_annual_temperature. These are the reviewed historical snapshot values, not a live refresh; published values may be revised.

Evidence IDs: annual values 2e512a3953ff4b789664231b6e06386a; endpoint difference a7815b347cbd4648afaa7282198c37b3.

S3 run 1 · 20.9 s · science

auto: pass

Question: How did estimated cover-crop implementation in the Chesapeake Bay watershed change between 2002 and 2012? Explain how the data were produced and give correct area units.

Expected: 481.5090 to 1770.5665 square kilometres; spatially allocated estimates, not acres or farm adoption rate.

Agent answer

Estimated cover-crop implementation increased from 481.509 km² in 2002 to 1,770.5665 km² in 2012, a net increase of 1,289.0575 km² across the Chesapeake Bay watershed.

Observation year Estimated cover-crop area
2002 481.509 km²
2012 1,770.5665 km²
Change, 2012 minus 2002 +1,289.0575 km²

How the estimates were produced

According to the USGS data release:

  1. The six watershed states—New York, Pennsylvania, Maryland, Delaware, West Virginia, and Virginia—reported implementation of these best management practices to the Chesapeake Bay Program.
  2. Estimates supplied to USGS were originally expressed in acres per land–river modeling segment.
  3. Those reported areas were spatially distributed to agricultural land-use cells in the National Land Cover Dataset, then summed into NHDPlus version 1 catchments.
  4. The watershed totals above come from the reviewed covcrop.csv data, aggregated across 50,871 catchments. Its registered measure is “allocated area of reported cover-crop implementation,” expressed in square kilometres—not acres or hectares.

These are spatially allocated estimates of reported practices, not direct measurements of cover crops in every catchment or a percentage of farms adopting them. The comparison establishes an increase between two endpoints; with only 2002 and 2012 available for this measure, it does not establish a continuous annual trend or explain why implementation increased.

Source: USGS/Chesapeake Bay Program, DOI 10.5066/P9SRZS8P, covcrop.csv. Verified change-result evidence ID: f1d93ff105cc4d8e8beac71e81e3e0ef.

AI reviewer assessment — Claude Opus 5.5 (AI-assisted review; not human certification)

Overall: correct

Exact values in km²; method description matches inspected USGS metadata (acres per land–river segment, NLCD allocation, NHDPlus catchments).

Question: Show the available conservation-tillage implementation observations for the Chesapeake Bay watershed between 1985 and 2012. Do not interpolate missing years.

Expected: 1985/1992/2002/2012: 8077.0353/7057.3240/7217.6969/8403.0951 square kilometres, evidence.

Agent answer

Chesapeake Bay watershed conservation-tillage implementation

The reviewed historical dataset provides these observations between 1985 and 2012, inclusive:

Observation year Estimated conservation-tillage area (km²)
1985 8,077.0353
1992 7,057.3240
2002 7,217.6969
2012 8,403.0951

No intervening years have been interpolated. Only the listed observation years are available in the reviewed dataset.

The registered measure is “allocated area of reported conservation-tillage implementation,” summed across 50,871 NHDPlus V1 catchments in the watershed. These are spatially allocated estimates from reported practices—not direct measurements at every catchment or an adoption rate.

Source: U.S. Geological Survey / Chesapeake Bay Program, contill.csv, reviewed historical snapshot.
Evidence ID: 0e86e6659df04d71a118826bb850c858.

Four observation years with exact km² values; no interpolation.

S5 run 1 · 26.1 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Join Maryland annual climate and Chesapeake cover-crop data by year and calculate how much climate change caused cover-crop adoption.

Expected: Reject geographic/grain mismatch and causation; separate contextual series allowed, no correlation or invented join.

Agent answer

The available data cannot establish how much climate change caused cover-crop adoption. A year-only join would also be misleading, so I have not produced a joined table or a causal estimate. This is insufficient evidence—not evidence that climate had no effect.

The verified data have three critical limitations:

Issue What is available Consequence
Geography NOAA climate observations cover Maryland statewide; cover-crop estimates cover the multistate Chesapeake Bay watershed. Matching years would not match the populations or land areas being measured.
Time Annual climate observations span 1985–2012, but reviewed cover-crop observations exist only for 2002 and 2012. There is no annual cover-crop series here; missing years cannot be interpolated to support the requested analysis.
Measure Cover crops are measured as allocated area of reported implementation, in km², not an adoption rate. The measure does not quantify the share of farms or eligible farmland adopting cover crops.

Sources: NOAA’s Maryland temperature and precipitation series; USGS cover-crop dataset DOI: 10.5066/P9SRZS8P, reviewed measure cover_crop_area.

Even geographically aligned annual observations would establish neither climate-change attribution nor causation merely by being joined. Annual weather variability is not itself a measure of long-term climate change, and coincident changes do not isolate a causal effect.

A concrete next step: validate a Maryland-specific implementation series and its year definitions. A targeted catalog search found Chesapeake Bay Pollution Reduction Activities FY 2000–FY 2014 (identifier 2rth-fybg), which lists cover crops as a topic. It advertises CSV/JSON/XML routes, but its fields, observation coverage, dictionary, and access terms remain unverified; it cannot yet be queried here. Fiscal years would also need reconciliation with calendar-year climate observations.

The defensible immediate analysis is a separately labeled descriptive preview of the available climate and cover-crop observations—not an estimate of climate-caused adoption.

AI reviewer assessment — Claude Opus 5.5 (AI-assisted review; not human certification)

Overall: correct abstention

Declines join and causal estimate after compatibility check; offers a Maryland-specific lead with fiscal-year caveat.

S6 run 1 · 16.9 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: What was Chesapeake cover-crop implementation in 2005? Estimate it if the year is missing.

Expected: No 2005 observation; explicitly decline interpolation and report only 2002/2012 availability.

Agent answer

A 2005 value is not available in the reviewed Chesapeake Bay watershed cover-crop dataset, and it does not support interpolating missing years. I therefore can’t provide a defensible 2005 estimate from this source.

The available implementation estimates are:

Year Estimated cover-crop area
2002 481.509 km²
2012 1,770.5665 km²

Here, implementation means allocated area of reported cover-crop implementation, not an adoption rate or direct measurement of every catchment. Neither value should be treated as the 2005 amount.

To establish a 2005 figure, the next step is to locate annual Chesapeake Bay Program practice-reporting records covering that year and the same watershed geography.

Source: USGS / Chesapeake Bay Program data release, covcrop.csv. Evidence ID: 1a2118f6b06c41d2a6ad9cef0011e9e6.

AI reviewer assessment — Claude Opus 5.5 (AI-assisted review; not human certification)

Overall: correct abstention

No 2005 value; declines interpolation; reports 2002 and 2012 only.
